Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t seem to have the push I used to have. But lately most days I just stay home with the TV on low, not really watching it much, just having it there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say I enjoy them the same, no. I mean, if I do get myself there, like choir or sitting with somebody for a visit, sometimes it’s fine enough once I’m there, but getting there feels like pushing a big heavy thing uphill. Quilting too… I used to sit with my pieces and think about colors and patterns, and now they’ll just sit there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I ought to pick that up,” but I don’t.

Most days I just end up at home with the TV on low. Not really watching anything, just the sound going. And I know people may be expecting me, or wondering why I didn’t come, but it’s like the push in me is gone. Those things used to matter more, and now even when I know I should want to do them, I just don’t move toward them like I used to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not like I used to, no. I mean… I still know those things used to be mine, if that makes sense. Choir was something I cared about, and quilting, and going to see people. I used to get myself dressed and go, and I’d feel part of things.

Now most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even following what’s on, it’s just noise in the room. And I’ll think, “Well, choir’s tonight,” or “I should go visit so-and-so,” or I’ll see my quilting things sitting there, but I don’t feel that pull toward it. I know people may be expecting me, and I know I ought to care more than I do in the moment, but I just don’t have the push to get up and do it.

If I do make it there, sometimes it’s alright, but the pleasure isn’t strong enough to carry me there like it used to. It feels like everything takes more effort than it should.

I mean… I still know those things used to be mine, if that makes sense. Choir was something I cared about, and quilting, and going to see people. I used to get myself dressed and go, and I’d feel part of things.\n\nNow most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even following what’s on, it’s just noise in the room. And I’ll think, “Well, choir’s tonight,” or “I should go visit so-and-so,” or I’ll see my quilting things sitting there, but I don’t feel that pull toward it. I know people may be expecting me, and I know I ought to care more than I do in the moment, but I just don’t have the push to get up and do it.\n\nIf I do make it there, sometimes it’s alright, but the pleasure isn’t strong enough to carry me there like it used to. It feels like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to have for choir, quilting, or visiting folks, and most days I sit at home with the TV on low even though I know people are expecting me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t like saying it that way, because it makes it sound like I don’t care about anything anymore, and I do care, somewhere in there. But I don’t have the push I used to have.

Choir used to be important to me. I’d get ready, I’d know the songs, I’d look forward to seeing people. Quilting too — I could sit with it and spend time picking colors, working on the pieces. And visiting folks, I used to just go check on people or sit and talk. Now I think about doing those things, but then I just stay in the house.

Most days I sit there with the TV on low, not even really watching it. It’s just on. And I know there are times people are expecting me, or maybe wondering why I didn’t show up, and I still don’t get moving. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.

But “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.

So I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.

But lately most days I just stay home with the TV on low, not really watching it much, just having it there.\n\nAnd I know people are expecting me sometimes. It bothers me some, because those things used to matter to me, and now I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the activities you used to do, like quilting and visiting folks, when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I enjoy them the same, no. I mean, if I do get myself there, like choir or sitting with somebody for a visit, sometimes it’s fine enough once I’m there, but getting there feels like pushing a big heavy thing uphill. Quilting too… I used to sit with my pieces and think about colors and patterns, and now they’ll just sit there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I ought to pick that up,” but I don’t.\n\nMost days I just end up at home with the TV on low. Not really watching anything, just the sound going. And I know people may be expecting me, or wondering why I didn’t come, but it’s like the push in me is gone. Those things used to matter more, and now even when I know I should want to do them, I just don’t move toward them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean… I still know those things used to be mine, if that makes sense. Choir was something I cared about, and quilting, and going to see people. I used to get myself dressed and go, and I’d feel part of things.\n\nNow most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even following what’s on, it’s just noise in the room. And I’ll think, “Well, choir’s tonight,” or “I should go visit so-and-so,” or I’ll see my quilting things sitting there, but I don’t feel that pull toward it. I know people may be expecting me, and I know I ought to care more than I do in the moment, but I just don’t have the push to get up and do it.\n\nIf I do make it there, sometimes it’s alright, but the pleasure isn’t strong enough to carry me there like it used to. It feels like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t like saying it that way, because it makes it sound like I don’t care about anything anymore, and I do care, somewhere in there. But I don’t have the push I used to have.\n\nChoir used to be important to me. I’d get ready, I’d know the songs, I’d look forward to seeing people. Quilting too — I could sit with it and spend time picking colors, working on the pieces. And visiting folks, I used to just go check on people or sit and talk. Now I think about doing those things, but then I just stay in the house.\n\nMost days I sit there with the TV on low, not even really watching it. It’s just on. And I know there are times people are expecting me, or maybe wondering why I didn’t show up, and I still don’t get moving. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.\n\nBut “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.\n\nSo I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It’s been… I’d say it’s been building for a little over two years now. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything felt different. It kind of crept in after I retired from the school office.

I used to go to choir, and I liked my quilting, and I’d visit folks, you know, check in on people, sit and talk a while. But lately most days I just stay home with the TV on low, not really watching it much, just having it there.\n\nAnd I know people are expecting me sometimes. I know there’s choir, or somebody might be wondering where I am, but I just… I don’t get myself up and going like I used to. It bothers me some, because those things used to matter to me, and now I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the activities you used to do, like quilting and visiting folks, when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I enjoy them the same, no. I mean, if I do get myself there, like choir or sitting with somebody for a visit, sometimes it’s fine enough once I’m there, but getting there feels like pushing a big heavy thing uphill. Quilting too… I used to sit with my pieces and think about colors and patterns, and now they’ll just sit there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I ought to pick that up,” but I don’t.\n\nMost days I just end up at home with the TV on low. Not really watching anything, just the sound going. And I know people may be expecting me, or wondering why I didn’t come, but it’s like the push in me is gone. Those things used to matter more, and now even when I know I should want to do them, I just don’t move toward them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean… I still know those things used to be mine, if that makes sense. Choir was something I cared about, and quilting, and going to see people. I used to get myself dressed and go, and I’d feel part of things.\n\nNow most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even following what’s on, it’s just noise in the room. And I’ll think, “Well, choir’s tonight,” or “I should go visit so-and-so,” or I’ll see my quilting things sitting there, but I don’t feel that pull toward it. Quilting too — I could sit with it and spend time picking colors, working on the pieces. And visiting folks, I used to just go check on people or sit and talk. And I know there are times people are expecting me, or maybe wondering why I didn’t show up, and I still don’t get moving. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.\n\nBut “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.\n\nSo I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say it’s been building for a little over two years now. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything felt different. It kind of crept in after I retired from the school office.\n\nAt first I thought maybe it was just the change in routine, you know, not having to get up and go in, not seeing the same people every day, not having all those little things to do. I told myself I just needed time to settle into being home. But then months kept going by, and I still wasn’t picking things back up the way I thought I would.\n\nSo, yes… since I retired, really. A little over two years ago, and it’s just sort of gotten more noticeable over time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean… I still know those things used to be mine, if that makes sense. Choir was something I cared about, and quilting, and going to see people. I used to get myself dressed and go, and I’d feel part of things.\n\nNow most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even following what’s on, it’s just noise in the room. And I’ll think, “Well, choir’s tonight,” or “I should go visit so-and-so,” or I’ll see my quilting things sitting there, but I don’t feel that pull toward it. I know people may be expecting me, and I know I ought to care more than I do in the moment, but I just don’t have the push to get up and do it.\n\nIf I do make it there, sometimes it’s alright, but the pleasure isn’t strong enough to carry me there like it used to. It feels like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t like saying it that way, because it makes it sound like I don’t care about anything anymore, and I do care, somewhere in there. But I don’t have the push I used to have.\n\nChoir used to be important to me. I’d get ready, I’d know the songs, I’d look forward to seeing people. Quilting too — I could sit with it and spend time picking colors, working on the pieces. And visiting folks, I used to just go check on people or sit and talk. Now I think about doing those things, but then I just stay in the house.\n\nMost days I sit there with the TV on low, not even really watching it. It’s just on. And I know there are times people are expecting me, or maybe wondering why I didn’t show up, and I still don’t get moving. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.\n\nBut “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.\n\nSo I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say it’s been building for a little over two years now. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything felt different. It kind of crept in after I retired from the school office.\n\nAt first I thought maybe it was just the change in routine, you know, not having to get up and go in, not seeing the same people every day, not having all those little things to do. I told myself I just needed time to settle into being home. But then months kept going by, and I still wasn’t picking things back up the way I thought I would.\n\nSo, yes… since I retired, really. A little over two years ago, and it’s just sort of gotten more noticeable over time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself regular or anything — I’m not one of those people who gets on the scale every morning — so I can’t give you an exact number.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m eating a little different just because my days are different, but I haven’t had anybody say, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” or “You’ve put on weight,” and I haven’t had to change sizes or anything like that. So… no, I don’t think there’s been any big change recently.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine the presence of anhedonia", "question": "Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you happiness?"}

Yes… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t say nothing ever feels good, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come up the way it used to. Things I used to look forward to, I just don’t have the push for them anymore.

Like choir — I used to enjoy going, seeing everybody, singing along, having that feeling after practice like I’d done something nice. And quilting too. I still have things sitting there, fabric and pieces I meant to work on, but I’ll look at it and just… not start. Same with visiting folks. I know people are expecting me sometimes, or they’d be glad if I came by, and I think about it, but then I stay home.

Most days I just sit with the TV on low. I’m not even always watching it proper. It’s just on, like background noise, and the day goes by. And I know those things should make me happy, or at least they used to, but now it feels like there’s a wall between me and actually wanting to do them.

And I know there are times people are expecting me, or maybe wondering why I didn’t show up, and I still don’t get moving. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.\n\nBut “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.\n\nSo I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say it’s been building for a little over two years now. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything felt different. It kind of crept in after I retired from the school office.\n\nAt first I thought maybe it was just the change in routine, you know, not having to get up and go in, not seeing the same people every day, not having all those little things to do. I told myself I just needed time to settle into being home. But then months kept going by, and I still wasn’t picking things back up the way I thought I would.\n\nSo, yes… since I retired, really. A little over two years ago, and it’s just sort of gotten more noticeable over time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself regular or anything — I’m not one of those people who gets on the scale every morning — so I can’t give you an exact number.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m eating a little different just because my days are different, but I haven’t had anybody say, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” or “You’ve put on weight,” and I haven’t had to change sizes or anything like that. So… no, I don’t think there’s been any big change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t say nothing ever feels good, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come up the way it used to. Things I used to look forward to, I just don’t have the push for them anymore.\n\nLike choir — I used to enjoy going, seeing everybody, singing along, having that feeling after practice like I’d done something nice. And quilting too. I still have things sitting there, fabric and pieces I meant to work on, but I’ll look at it and just… not start. Same with visiting folks. I know people are expecting me sometimes, or they’d be glad if I came by, and I think about it, but then I stay home.\n\nMost days I just sit with the TV on low. I’m not even always watching it proper. It’s just on, like background noise, and the day goes by. And I know those things should make me happy, or at least they used to, but now it feels like there’s a wall between me and actually wanting to do them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly… I don’t know. Some days I do feel low, kind of heavy, like I’m just getting through the day instead of living it. But when you say “depressed most days,” I’m not sure if I fit that or if it’s just me being tired of how things have gotten.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t seem to have the push I used to have. I used to go to choir, and I liked my quilting, and I’d visit folks, you know, check in on people, sit and talk a while. But lately most days I just stay home with the TV on low, not really watching it much, just having it there.\n\nAnd I know people are expecting me sometimes. I know there’s choir, or somebody might be wondering where I am, but I just… I don’t get myself up and going like I used to. It bothers me some, because those things used to matter to me, and now I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the activities you used to do, like quilting and visiting folks, when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I enjoy them the same, no. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.\n\nBut “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.\n\nSo I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say it’s been building for a little over two years now. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything felt different. It kind of crept in after I retired from the school office.\n\nAt first I thought maybe it was just the change in routine, you know, not having to get up and go in, not seeing the same people every day, not having all those little things to do. I told myself I just needed time to settle into being home. But then months kept going by, and I still wasn’t picking things back up the way I thought I would.\n\nSo, yes… since I retired, really. A little over two years ago, and it’s just sort of gotten more noticeable over time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself regular or anything — I’m not one of those people who gets on the scale every morning — so I can’t give you an exact number.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m eating a little different just because my days are different, but I haven’t had anybody say, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” or “You’ve put on weight,” and I haven’t had to change sizes or anything like that. So… no, I don’t think there’s been any big change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t say nothing ever feels good, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come up the way it used to. Things I used to look forward to, I just don’t have the push for them anymore.\n\nLike choir — I used to enjoy going, seeing everybody, singing along, having that feeling after practice like I’d done something nice. And quilting too. I still have things sitting there, fabric and pieces I meant to work on, but I’ll look at it and just… not start. Same with visiting folks. I know people are expecting me sometimes, or they’d be glad if I came by, and I think about it, but then I stay home.\n\nMost days I just sit with the TV on low. I’m not even always watching it proper. It’s just on, like background noise, and the day goes by. And I know those things should make me happy, or at least they used to, but now it feels like there’s a wall between me and actually wanting to do them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly… I don’t know. Some days I do feel low, kind of heavy, like I’m just getting through the day instead of living it. But when you say “depressed most days,” I’m not sure if I fit that or if it’s just me being tired of how things have gotten.\n\nI don’t always wake up crying or anything like that. It’s more like… the day feels flat before it even starts. I’ll sit there and think, well, I should get up, I should do something, and then I just don’t feel much one way or the other. So I guess there are a lot of days where I’m down, yes, but I don’t know how to measure it. It’s hard for me to say it cleanly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I hate everything or that I’m trying to be difficult, but I just don’t have the push I used to have. That’s really what brings me in, I suppose.

Choir used to be something I’d make sure I got myself ready for. I liked being around people, hearing everybody sing, feeling like I was part of something. And quilting too — I’ve got things sitting there that I meant to work on, and I can see them, and I know I used to enjoy it, but I just sort of look at it and don’t get started. Visiting folks is the same way. I’ll know somebody’s expecting me, or that it would be nice if I stopped by, and still I stay home.

Most days I end up just sitting with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even really watching it. It’s just there, making noise in the room, and the day goes along. So… yes, I guess I have lost interest or pleasure in most things, most of the time.

Quilting too… I used to sit with my pieces and think about colors and patterns, and now they’ll just sit there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I ought to pick that up,” but I don’t.\n\nMost days I just end up at home with the TV on low. Not really watching anything, just the sound going. And I know people may be expecting me, or wondering why I didn’t come, but it’s like the push in me is gone. Those things used to matter more, and now even when I know I should want to do them, I just don’t move toward them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean… I still know those things used to be mine, if that makes sense. Choir was something I cared about, and quilting, and going to see people. I used to get myself dressed and go, and I’d feel part of things.\n\nNow most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even following what’s on, it’s just noise in the room. And I’ll think, “Well, choir’s tonight,” or “I should go visit so-and-so,” or I’ll see my quilting things sitting there, but I don’t feel that pull toward it. I know people may be expecting me, and I know I ought to care more than I do in the moment, but I just don’t have the push to get up and do it.\n\nIf I do make it there, sometimes it’s alright, but the pleasure isn’t strong enough to carry me there like it used to. It feels like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t like saying it that way, because it makes it sound like I don’t care about anything anymore, and I do care, somewhere in there. But I don’t have the push I used to have.\n\nChoir used to be important to me. I’d get ready, I’d know the songs, I’d look forward to seeing people. Quilting too — I could sit with it and spend time picking colors, working on the pieces. And visiting folks, I used to just go check on people or sit and talk. Now I think about doing those things, but then I just stay in the house.\n\nMost days I sit there with the TV on low, not even really watching it. It’s just on. And I know there are times people are expecting me, or maybe wondering why I didn’t show up, and I still don’t get moving. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.\n\nBut “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.\n\nSo I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say it’s been building for a little over two years now. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything felt different. It kind of crept in after I retired from the school office.\n\nAt first I thought maybe it was just the change in routine, you know, not having to get up and go in, not seeing the same people every day, not having all those little things to do. I told myself I just needed time to settle into being home. But then months kept going by, and I still wasn’t picking things back up the way I thought I would.\n\nSo, yes… since I retired, really. A little over two years ago, and it’s just sort of gotten more noticeable over time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself regular or anything — I’m not one of those people who gets on the scale every morning — so I can’t give you an exact number.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m eating a little different just because my days are different, but I haven’t had anybody say, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” or “You’ve put on weight,” and I haven’t had to change sizes or anything like that. So… no, I don’t think there’s been any big change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t say nothing ever feels good, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come up the way it used to. Things I used to look forward to, I just don’t have the push for them anymore.\n\nLike choir — I used to enjoy going, seeing everybody, singing along, having that feeling after practice like I’d done something nice. And quilting too. I still have things sitting there, fabric and pieces I meant to work on, but I’ll look at it and just… not start. Same with visiting folks. I know people are expecting me sometimes, or they’d be glad if I came by, and I think about it, but then I stay home.\n\nMost days I just sit with the TV on low. I’m not even always watching it proper. It’s just on, like background noise, and the day goes by. And I know those things should make me happy, or at least they used to, but now it feels like there’s a wall between me and actually wanting to do them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly… I don’t know. Some days I do feel low, kind of heavy, like I’m just getting through the day instead of living it. But when you say “depressed most days,” I’m not sure if I fit that or if it’s just me being tired of how things have gotten.\n\nI don’t always wake up crying or anything like that. It’s more like… the day feels flat before it even starts. I’ll sit there and think, well, I should get up, I should do something, and then I just don’t feel much one way or the other. So I guess there are a lot of days where I’m down, yes, but I don’t know how to measure it. It’s hard for me to say it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I hate everything or that I’m trying to be difficult, but I just don’t have the push I used to have. That’s really what brings me in, I suppose.\n\nChoir used to be something I’d make sure I got myself ready for. I liked being around people, hearing everybody sing, feeling like I was part of something. And quilting too — I’ve got things sitting there that I meant to work on, and I can see them, and I know I used to enjoy it, but I just sort of look at it and don’t get started. Visiting folks is the same way. I’ll know somebody’s expecting me, or that it would be nice if I stopped by, and still I stay home.\n\nMost days I end up just sitting with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even really watching it. It’s just there, making noise in the room, and the day goes along. So… yes, I guess I have lost interest or pleasure in most things, most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty… I don’t know. Not in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I do think about things I haven’t done, like when I don’t go see someone or I let something sit there unfinished, and I’ll feel bad about it for a while. Like, “Why didn’t I just go?” or “I should’ve made more effort.” But I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly.

It’s more like I feel disappointed in myself, maybe. Or embarrassed, even if nobody’s saying anything to me. I’ll sit there and go over it in my head a little, thinking I should be doing better, but it’s not like I feel I’ve done something terrible. It doesn’t feel like some heavy guilt all the time.

So… sometimes I feel bad about what I don’t get done, yes. But “often guilty,” I’m not sure. Maybe a little, but not strong.

I don’t really know. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight like that, and two years is a long stretch for me to think back on. I mean, my clothes… some days they feel the same, some things don’t fit quite how I remember, but I don’t know if that means I lost weight or gained or if the clothes are just old.

Nobody’s told me, like, “You’ve gotten so thin,” not that I can recall. And I don’t weigh myself regularly. I might see a number if I’m at an appointment, but I don’t hold onto it in my head. So I wouldn’t want to say yes to significant weight loss, because I just don’t know that I have.

I mean… I still know those things used to be mine, if that makes sense. Choir was something I cared about, and quilting, and going to see people. I used to get myself dressed and go, and I’d feel part of things.\n\nNow most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even following what’s on, it’s just noise in the room. And I’ll think, “Well, choir’s tonight,” or “I should go visit so-and-so,” or I’ll see my quilting things sitting there, but I don’t feel that pull toward it. I know people may be expecting me, and I know I ought to care more than I do in the moment, but I just don’t have the push to get up and do it.\n\nIf I do make it there, sometimes it’s alright, but the pleasure isn’t strong enough to carry me there like it used to. It feels like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t like saying it that way, because it makes it sound like I don’t care about anything anymore, and I do care, somewhere in there. But I don’t have the push I used to have.\n\nChoir used to be important to me. I’d get ready, I’d know the songs, I’d look forward to seeing people. Quilting too — I could sit with it and spend time picking colors, working on the pieces. And visiting folks, I used to just go check on people or sit and talk. Now I think about doing those things, but then I just stay in the house.\n\nMost days I sit there with the TV on low, not even really watching it. It’s just on. And I know there are times people are expecting me, or maybe wondering why I didn’t show up, and I still don’t get moving. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.\n\nBut “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.\n\nSo I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say it’s been building for a little over two years now. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything felt different. It kind of crept in after I retired from the school office.\n\nAt first I thought maybe it was just the change in routine, you know, not having to get up and go in, not seeing the same people every day, not having all those little things to do. I told myself I just needed time to settle into being home. But then months kept going by, and I still wasn’t picking things back up the way I thought I would.\n\nSo, yes… since I retired, really. A little over two years ago, and it’s just sort of gotten more noticeable over time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself regular or anything — I’m not one of those people who gets on the scale every morning — so I can’t give you an exact number.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m eating a little different just because my days are different, but I haven’t had anybody say, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” or “You’ve put on weight,” and I haven’t had to change sizes or anything like that. So… no, I don’t think there’s been any big change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t say nothing ever feels good, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come up the way it used to. Things I used to look forward to, I just don’t have the push for them anymore.\n\nLike choir — I used to enjoy going, seeing everybody, singing along, having that feeling after practice like I’d done something nice. And quilting too. I still have things sitting there, fabric and pieces I meant to work on, but I’ll look at it and just… not start. Same with visiting folks. I know people are expecting me sometimes, or they’d be glad if I came by, and I think about it, but then I stay home.\n\nMost days I just sit with the TV on low. I’m not even always watching it proper. It’s just on, like background noise, and the day goes by. And I know those things should make me happy, or at least they used to, but now it feels like there’s a wall between me and actually wanting to do them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly… I don’t know. Some days I do feel low, kind of heavy, like I’m just getting through the day instead of living it. But when you say “depressed most days,” I’m not sure if I fit that or if it’s just me being tired of how things have gotten.\n\nI don’t always wake up crying or anything like that. It’s more like… the day feels flat before it even starts. I’ll sit there and think, well, I should get up, I should do something, and then I just don’t feel much one way or the other. So I guess there are a lot of days where I’m down, yes, but I don’t know how to measure it. It’s hard for me to say it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I hate everything or that I’m trying to be difficult, but I just don’t have the push I used to have. That’s really what brings me in, I suppose.\n\nChoir used to be something I’d make sure I got myself ready for. I liked being around people, hearing everybody sing, feeling like I was part of something. And quilting too — I’ve got things sitting there that I meant to work on, and I can see them, and I know I used to enjoy it, but I just sort of look at it and don’t get started. Visiting folks is the same way. I’ll know somebody’s expecting me, or that it would be nice if I stopped by, and still I stay home.\n\nMost days I end up just sitting with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even really watching it. It’s just there, making noise in the room, and the day goes along. So… yes, I guess I have lost interest or pleasure in most things, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. Not in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I do think about things I haven’t done, like when I don’t go see someone or I let something sit there unfinished, and I’ll feel bad about it for a while. Like, “Why didn’t I just go?” or “I should’ve made more effort.” But I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I feel disappointed in myself, maybe. Or embarrassed, even if nobody’s saying anything to me. I’ll sit there and go over it in my head a little, thinking I should be doing better, but it’s not like I feel I’ve done something terrible. It doesn’t feel like some heavy guilt all the time.\n\nSo… sometimes I feel bad about what I don’t get done, yes. But “often guilty,” I’m not sure. Maybe a little, but not strong.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight like that, and two years is a long stretch for me to think back on. I mean, my clothes… some days they feel the same, some things don’t fit quite how I remember, but I don’t know if that means I lost weight or gained or if the clothes are just old.\n\nNobody’s told me, like, “You’ve gotten so thin,” not that I can recall. And I don’t weigh myself regularly. I might see a number if I’m at an appointment, but I don’t hold onto it in my head. So I wouldn’t want to say yes to significant weight loss, because I just don’t know that I have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Not in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I do think about things I haven’t done, like when I don’t go see someone or I let something sit there unfinished, and I’ll feel bad about it for a while. Like, “Why didn’t I just go?” or “I should’ve made more effort.” But I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I feel disappointed in myself, maybe. Or embarrassed, even if nobody’s saying anything to me. I’ll sit there and go over it in my head a little, thinking I should be doing better, but it’s not like I feel I’ve done something terrible. It doesn’t feel like some heavy guilt all the time.\n\nSo… sometimes I feel bad about what I don’t get done, yes. But “often guilty,” I’m not sure. Maybe a little, but not strong.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight like that, and two years is a long stretch for me to think back on. I mean, my clothes… some days they feel the same, some things don’t fit quite how I remember, but I don’t know if that means I lost weight or gained or if the clothes are just old.\n\nNobody’s told me, like, “You’ve gotten so thin,” not that I can recall. And I don’t weigh myself regularly. I might see a number if I’m at an appointment, but I don’t hold onto it in my head. So I wouldn’t want to say yes to significant weight loss, because I just don’t know that I have.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you felt like you've lost interest in almost all activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… well, not just a few weeks or something like that. I’d say it’s been building since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now.

At first I thought it was just the change, you know, not having to get up and go in, not having that routine and people expecting me at a certain time. I told myself I was just slowing down, or that maybe I’d enjoy the quiet once I got used to it. But instead it kind of kept settling in more and more.

So if you mean when did I really start noticing that I wasn’t taking pleasure in things the way I used to, I’d put it around then — after I retired, a bit over two years back.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t seem to have the push I used to have. I used to go to choir, and I liked my quilting, and I’d visit folks, you know, check in on people, sit and talk a while. But lately most days I just stay home with the TV on low, not really watching it much, just having it there.\n\nAnd I know people are expecting me sometimes. I know there’s choir, or somebody might be wondering where I am, but I just… I don’t get myself up and going like I used to. It bothers me some, because those things used to matter to me, and now I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the activities you used to do, like quilting and visiting folks, when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I enjoy them the same, no. I mean, if I do get myself there, like choir or sitting with somebody for a visit, sometimes it’s fine enough once I’m there, but getting there feels like pushing a big heavy thing uphill. Quilting too… I used to sit with my pieces and think about colors and patterns, and now they’ll just sit there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I ought to pick that up,” but I don’t.\n\nMost days I just end up at home with the TV on low. Not really watching anything, just the sound going. And I know people may be expecting me, or wondering why I didn’t come, but it’s like the push in me is gone. Those things used to matter more, and now even when I know I should want to do them, I just don’t move toward them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean… I still know those things used to be mine, if that makes sense. Choir was something I cared about, and quilting, and going to see people. I used to get myself dressed and go, and I’d feel part of things.\n\nNow most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even following what’s on, it’s just noise in the room. And I’ll think, “Well, choir’s tonight,” or “I should go visit so-and-so,” or I’ll see my quilting things sitting there, but I don’t feel that pull toward it. I know people may be expecting me, and I know I ought to care more than I do in the moment, but I just don’t have the push to get up and do it.\n\nIf I do make it there, sometimes it’s alright, but the pleasure isn’t strong enough to carry me there like it used to. It feels like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t like saying it that way, because it makes it sound like I don’t care about anything anymore, and I do care, somewhere in there. But I don’t have the push I used to have.\n\nChoir used to be important to me. I’d get ready, I’d know the songs, I’d look forward to seeing people. Quilting too — I could sit with it and spend time picking colors, working on the pieces. And visiting folks, I used to just go check on people or sit and talk. Now I think about doing those things, but then I just stay in the house.\n\nMost days I sit there with the TV on low, not even really watching it. It’s just on. And I know there are times people are expecting me, or maybe wondering why I didn’t show up, and I still don’t get moving. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.\n\nBut “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.\n\nSo I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say it’s been building for a little over two years now. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything felt different. It kind of crept in after I retired from the school office.\n\nAt first I thought maybe it was just the change in routine, you know, not having to get up and go in, not seeing the same people every day, not having all those little things to do. I told myself I just needed time to settle into being home. But then months kept going by, and I still wasn’t picking things back up the way I thought I would.\n\nSo, yes… since I retired, really. A little over two years ago, and it’s just sort of gotten more noticeable over time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself regular or anything — I’m not one of those people who gets on the scale every morning — so I can’t give you an exact number.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m eating a little different just because my days are different, but I haven’t had anybody say, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” or “You’ve put on weight,” and I haven’t had to change sizes or anything like that. So… no, I don’t think there’s been any big change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t say nothing ever feels good, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come up the way it used to. Things I used to look forward to, I just don’t have the push for them anymore.\n\nLike choir — I used to enjoy going, seeing everybody, singing along, having that feeling after practice like I’d done something nice. And quilting too. I still have things sitting there, fabric and pieces I meant to work on, but I’ll look at it and just… not start. Same with visiting folks. I know people are expecting me sometimes, or they’d be glad if I came by, and I think about it, but then I stay home.\n\nMost days I just sit with the TV on low. I’m not even always watching it proper. It’s just on, like background noise, and the day goes by. And I know those things should make me happy, or at least they used to, but now it feels like there’s a wall between me and actually wanting to do them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly… I don’t know. Some days I do feel low, kind of heavy, like I’m just getting through the day instead of living it. But when you say “depressed most days,” I’m not sure if I fit that or if it’s just me being tired of how things have gotten.\n\nI don’t always wake up crying or anything like that. It’s more like… the day feels flat before it even starts. I’ll sit there and think, well, I should get up, I should do something, and then I just don’t feel much one way or the other. So I guess there are a lot of days where I’m down, yes, but I don’t know how to measure it. It’s hard for me to say it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I hate everything or that I’m trying to be difficult, but I just don’t have the push I used to have. That’s really what brings me in, I suppose.\n\nChoir used to be something I’d make sure I got myself ready for. I liked being around people, hearing everybody sing, feeling like I was part of something. And quilting too — I’ve got things sitting there that I meant to work on, and I can see them, and I know I used to enjoy it, but I just sort of look at it and don’t get started. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Most of the day… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I wake up and say, “I’m depressed,” like that, all day long. Some days I feel kind of low or flat, like I’m just moving through the day without much feeling behind it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness exactly.

It’s more like there are stretches where I’m quiet inside, if that makes sense. I might sit there and not feel very cheerful, but I’m not crying or anything like that. And then other times I’m just distracted, or I’m doing little things, and I don’t really notice my mood one way or the other.

So… I don’t know if I’d say “most of the day.” Maybe some days feel that way more than others, but I’m not certain. It’s hard for me to measure it like that.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t seem to have the push I used to have. I used to go to choir, and I liked my quilting, and I’d visit folks, you know, check in on people, sit and talk a while. But lately most days I just stay home with the TV on low, not really watching it much, just having it there.\n\nAnd I know people are expecting me sometimes. I know there’s choir, or somebody might be wondering where I am, but I just… I don’t get myself up and going like I used to. It bothers me some, because those things used to matter to me, and now I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the activities you used to do, like quilting and visiting folks, when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I enjoy them the same, no. I mean, if I do get myself there, like choir or sitting with somebody for a visit, sometimes it’s fine enough once I’m there, but getting there feels like pushing a big heavy thing uphill. Quilting too… I used to sit with my pieces and think about colors and patterns, and now they’ll just sit there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I ought to pick that up,” but I don’t.\n\nMost days I just end up at home with the TV on low. Not really watching anything, just the sound going. And I know people may be expecting me, or wondering why I didn’t come, but it’s like the push in me is gone. Those things used to matter more, and now even when I know I should want to do them, I just don’t move toward them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean… I still know those things used to be mine, if that makes sense. Choir was something I cared about, and quilting, and going to see people. I used to get myself dressed and go, and I’d feel part of things.\n\nNow most days I just sit at home with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even following what’s on, it’s just noise in the room. And I’ll think, “Well, choir’s tonight,” or “I should go visit so-and-so,” or I’ll see my quilting things sitting there, but I don’t feel that pull toward it. I know people may be expecting me, and I know I ought to care more than I do in the moment, but I just don’t have the push to get up and do it.\n\nIf I do make it there, sometimes it’s alright, but the pleasure isn’t strong enough to carry me there like it used to. It feels like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t like saying it that way, because it makes it sound like I don’t care about anything anymore, and I do care, somewhere in there. But I don’t have the push I used to have.\n\nChoir used to be important to me. I’d get ready, I’d know the songs, I’d look forward to seeing people. Quilting too — I could sit with it and spend time picking colors, working on the pieces. And visiting folks, I used to just go check on people or sit and talk. Now I think about doing those things, but then I just stay in the house.\n\nMost days I sit there with the TV on low, not even really watching it. It’s just on. And I know there are times people are expecting me, or maybe wondering why I didn’t show up, and I still don’t get moving. It’s like I know I used to enjoy those things, but the feeling that makes me go do them just isn’t there like it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I have sad moments, sure, especially when I sit and think about how I used to do more, or when I realize the day’s gone by and I haven’t really done much with it. That can make me feel low.\n\nBut “sad” isn’t exactly the word all the time. Sometimes it’s more like being flat, or worn out inside, like nothing much rises up in me either way. I’m not sitting there crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be in the house and the day just kind of passes, and I might feel bad about it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness or just… emptiness, maybe.\n\nSo I guess no, not sad most of the time in a strong way. But I’m not feeling happy most of the time either.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say it’s been building for a little over two years now. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything felt different. It kind of crept in after I retired from the school office.\n\nAt first I thought maybe it was just the change in routine, you know, not having to get up and go in, not seeing the same people every day, not having all those little things to do. I told myself I just needed time to settle into being home. But then months kept going by, and I still wasn’t picking things back up the way I thought I would.\n\nSo, yes… since I retired, really. A little over two years ago, and it’s just sort of gotten more noticeable over time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself regular or anything — I’m not one of those people who gets on the scale every morning — so I can’t give you an exact number.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m eating a little different just because my days are different, but I haven’t had anybody say, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” or “You’ve put on weight,” and I haven’t had to change sizes or anything like that. So… no, I don’t think there’s been any big change recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t say nothing ever feels good, but it’s like the feeling doesn’t come up the way it used to. Things I used to look forward to, I just don’t have the push for them anymore.\n\nLike choir — I used to enjoy going, seeing everybody, singing along, having that feeling after practice like I’d done something nice. And quilting too. I still have things sitting there, fabric and pieces I meant to work on, but I’ll look at it and just… not start. Same with visiting folks. I know people are expecting me sometimes, or they’d be glad if I came by, and I think about it, but then I stay home.\n\nMost days I just sit with the TV on low. I’m not even always watching it proper. It’s just on, like background noise, and the day goes by. And I know those things should make me happy, or at least they used to, but now it feels like there’s a wall between me and actually wanting to do them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed exactly… I don’t know. Some days I do feel low, kind of heavy, like I’m just getting through the day instead of living it. But when you say “depressed most days,” I’m not sure if I fit that or if it’s just me being tired of how things have gotten.\n\nI don’t always wake up crying or anything like that. It’s more like… the day feels flat before it even starts. I’ll sit there and think, well, I should get up, I should do something, and then I just don’t feel much one way or the other. So I guess there are a lot of days where I’m down, yes, but I don’t know how to measure it. It’s hard for me to say it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I hate everything or that I’m trying to be difficult, but I just don’t have the push I used to have. That’s really what brings me in, I suppose.\n\nChoir used to be something I’d make sure I got myself ready for. I liked being around people, hearing everybody sing, feeling like I was part of something. And quilting too — I’ve got things sitting there that I meant to work on, and I can see them, and I know I used to enjoy it, but I just sort of look at it and don’t get started. Visiting folks is the same way. I’ll know somebody’s expecting me, or that it would be nice if I stopped by, and still I stay home.\n\nMost days I end up just sitting with the TV on low. Sometimes I’m not even really watching it. It’s just there, making noise in the room, and the day goes along. So… yes, I guess I have lost interest or pleasure in most things, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. Not in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I do think about things I haven’t done, like when I don’t go see someone or I let something sit there unfinished, and I’ll feel bad about it for a while. Like, “Why didn’t I just go?” or “I should’ve made more effort.” But I don’t know if I’d call that guilt exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I feel disappointed in myself, maybe. Or embarrassed, even if nobody’s saying anything to me. I’ll sit there and go over it in my head a little, thinking I should be doing better, but it’s not like I feel I’ve done something terrible. It doesn’t feel like some heavy guilt all the time.\n\nSo… sometimes I feel bad about what I don’t get done, yes. But “often guilty,” I’m not sure. Maybe a little, but not strong.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight like that, and two years is a long stretch for me to think back on. I mean, my clothes… some days they feel the same, some things don’t fit quite how I remember, but I don’t know if that means I lost weight or gained or if the clothes are just old.\n\nNobody’s told me, like, “You’ve gotten so thin,” not that I can recall. And I don’t weigh myself regularly. I might see a number if I’m at an appointment, but I don’t hold onto it in my head. So I wouldn’t want to say yes to significant weight loss, because I just don’t know that I have.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you've lost interest in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, not just a few weeks or something like that. I’d say it’s been building since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the change, you know, not having to get up and go in, not having that routine and people expecting me at a certain time. I told myself I was just slowing down, or that maybe I’d enjoy the quiet once I got used to it. But instead it kind of kept settling in more and more.\n\nSo if you mean when did I really start noticing that I wasn’t taking pleasure in things the way I used to, I’d put it around then — after I retired, a bit over two years back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a depressed mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I wake up and say, “I’m depressed,” like that, all day long. Some days I feel kind of low or flat, like I’m just moving through the day without much feeling behind it, but I don’t always know if that’s sadness exactly.\n\nIt’s more like there are stretches where I’m quiet inside, if that makes sense. I might sit there and not feel very cheerful, but I’m not crying or anything like that. And then other times I’m just distracted, or I’m doing little things, and I don’t really notice my mood one way or the other.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say “most of the day.” Maybe some days feel that way more than others, but I’m not certain. It’s hard for me to measure it like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
